Well, doc, what brings me in is... I guess it's my eating habits lately. See, I work these late shifts down at the plant—mostly line work, checking the seals on the packaging, which is mind-numbing, let me tell you. The guy next to me, Frank, he always complains about his back, but honestly, my back is fine. It's just the hours that get to you. Anyway, after those late shifts, I'll stop for takeout on the way home. I end up getting, like, two burgers, a large fry, a shake... and you'd think that'd be enough, right? But then I get home, and I go straight to the extra fridge we keep out in the garage. My wife wanted that fridge for Thanksgiving turkeys, but she barely uses it. So I'll stand out there in the garage and just clean out whatever leftovers are in there, like it's a whole second supper. It's just... I don't know, it's getting out of hand.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Control? No, not really. It’s more like... once I start, it feels like a switch is flipped. I’ll be standing out there in the cold, literally telling myself, 'Roy, shut the bag and go inside,' but my hands just keep reaching for more. 

And usually it's those giant bulk bags, you know? My wife, Linda, she gets them at Costco. She goes every other Saturday with her sister, Patty. They make a whole day of it, always get the hot dogs at the food court. I keep telling her, 'Linda, the kids are grown, it's just the two of us now, we don't need a fifty-pound bag of rice or those massive tubs of pretzels.' But she insists it’s a good deal because of the unit price. The salt from those pretzels is a nightmare, though—it gets all over the concrete out there. I actually had to sweep the whole garage floor last Sunday because it was tracking into the mudroom and scratching up the linoleum. 

But, uh, what was I... right, the eating. Yeah, even when I know I should just zip the bag up and walk away, my hands just keep going back for another handful.

Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. Afterward, I just stand there looking at the empty wrappers and I feel... I don't know, ashamed. Disgusted with myself, really. I mean, I've always been the dependable one, you know? Thirty years at the plant, never missed a shift unless I had a fever over 102. 

Speaking of the plant, they just changed our punch-in system. Used to be these heavy paper cards—you'd hear that solid *clack* when you punched in. Now it's all digital badges. You just wave it near a sensor, and it makes this pathetic little beep. Half the time the sensor doesn't even read it right, and then you have to go see Brenda in HR. Brenda's nice enough, I guess, but she keeps her office freezing cold. She's got this little space heater under her desk, but it doesn't do a thing for the rest of the room. I was in there for twenty minutes last Tuesday trying to fix a missed punch, and my hands were practically numb by the time I left. 

But, anyway, yeah, the eating. It just makes me feel like I'm sneaking around in my own house, you know? Like I'm letting myself down. I'm supposed to be disciplined, but out there in the garage... I just lose it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, what brings me in is... I guess it's my eating habits lately. See, I work these late shifts down at the plant—mostly line work, checking the seals on the packaging, which is mind-numbing, let me tell you. The guy next to me, Frank, he always complains about his back, but honestly, my back is fine. It's just the hours that get to you. Anyway, after those late shifts, I'll stop for takeout on the way home. I end up getting, like, two burgers, a large fry, a shake... and you'd think that'd be enough, right? But then I get home, and I go straight to the extra fridge we keep out in the garage. My wife wanted that fridge for Thanksgiving turkeys, but she barely uses it. So I'll stand out there in the garage and just clean out whatever leftovers are in there, like it's a whole second supper. It's just... I don't know, it's getting out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating that second supper in the garage?\n\nPatient: Control? No, not really. It’s more like... once I start, it feels like a switch is flipped. I’ll be standing out there in the cold, literally telling myself, 'Roy, shut the bag and go inside,' but my hands just keep reaching for more. \n\nAnd usually it's those giant bulk bags, you know? My wife, Linda, she gets them at Costco. She goes every other Saturday with her sister, Patty. They make a whole day of it, always get the hot dogs at the food court. I keep telling her, 'Linda, the kids are grown, it's just the two of us now, we don't need a fifty-pound bag of rice or those massive tubs of pretzels.' But she insists it’s a good deal because of the unit price. The salt from those pretzels is a nightmare, though—it gets all over the concrete out there. I actually had to sweep the whole garage floor last Sunday because it was tracking into the mudroom and scratching up the linoleum. \n\nBut, uh, what was I... right, the eating. Yeah, even when I know I should just zip the bag up and walk away, my hands just keep going back for another handful.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes in the garage?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. Afterward, I just stand there looking at the empty wrappers and I feel... I don't know, ashamed. Disgusted with myself, really. I mean, I've always been the dependable one, you know? Thirty years at the plant, never missed a shift unless I had a fever over 102. \n\nSpeaking of the plant, they just changed our punch-in system. Used to be these heavy paper cards—you'd hear that solid *clack* when you punched in. Now it's all digital badges. You just wave it near a sensor, and it makes this pathetic little beep. Half the time the sensor doesn't even read it right, and then you have to go see Brenda in HR. Brenda's nice enough, I guess, but she keeps her office freezing cold. She's got this little space heater under her desk, but it doesn't do a thing for the rest of the room. I was in there for twenty minutes last Tuesday trying to fix a missed punch, and my hands were practically numb by the time I left. \n\nBut, anyway, yeah, the eating. It just makes me feel like I'm sneaking around in my own house, you know? Like I'm letting myself down. I'm supposed to be disciplined, but out there in the garage... I just lose it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you usually feel hungry when you start eating that second supper in the garage?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Hungry? I mean... I don't really know if it's hunger. It's more just... I'm there, and the food's there. Honestly, half the time my stomach is already tight from the takeout I had on the drive home. 

You know, the drive home is supposed to be the relaxing part of the night. But they've been doing construction on Route 9 for... gosh, it feels like three years now. They put up those orange barrels, and then nobody ever seems to actually be working. Just miles of barrels. Last week, this guy in a silver Honda tried to cut me off right where it merges down to one lane. I honked at him, and he had the nerve to flip me the bird! Can you believe that? People just have no patience anymore. My dad used to say that how a man drives tells you everything you need to know about his character. Dad drove a '78 Ford pickup, manual transmission. He taught me how to drive stick in the parking lot of the old abandoned mall on the east side. That mall used to be the place to go, you know? They had a Sears, a JCPenney, and this incredible arcade where I spent half my allowance on Pac-Man. 

But, uh... what were you asking? Oh, right, the hunger. Yeah, I don't think I'm actually hungry when I'm out in the garage. I just... I just eat anyway.

I actually had to sweep the whole garage floor last Sunday because it was tracking into the mudroom and scratching up the linoleum. \n\nBut, uh, what was I... right, the eating. I mean, I've always been the dependable one, you know? Thirty years at the plant, never missed a shift unless I had a fever over 102. \n\nSpeaking of the plant, they just changed our punch-in system. Used to be these heavy paper cards—you'd hear that solid *clack* when you punched in. Now it's all digital badges. You just wave it near a sensor, and it makes this pathetic little beep. Half the time the sensor doesn't even read it right, and then you have to go see Brenda in HR. Brenda's nice enough, I guess, but she keeps her office freezing cold. She's got this little space heater under her desk, but it doesn't do a thing for the rest of the room. I was in there for twenty minutes last Tuesday trying to fix a missed punch, and my hands were practically numb by the time I left. \n\nBut, anyway, yeah, the eating. It just makes me feel like I'm sneaking around in my own house, you know? Like I'm letting myself down. I'm supposed to be disciplined, but out there in the garage... I just lose it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry when you start eating that second supper in the garage?\n\nPatient: Hungry? I mean... I don't really know if it's hunger. It's more just... I'm there, and the food's there. Last week, this guy in a silver Honda tried to cut me off right where it merges down to one lane. I honked at him, and he had the nerve to flip me the bird! Can you believe that? People just have no patience anymore. My dad used to say that how a man drives tells you everything you need to know about his character. Dad drove a '78 Ford pickup, manual transmission. He taught me how to drive stick in the parking lot of the old abandoned mall on the east side. That mall used to be the place to go, you know? Stressed? Anxious? I... I don't really know if I'd call it that. I don't feel like I'm upset when I go out there. It's just sort of my routine now, you know? After work, I pull in, and it's just what I do.

months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about five months, since I was put in the acting lead role on the CNC line.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / production_worker / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S049\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes in the garage?\n\nPatient: Guilty? People just have no patience anymore. My dad used to say that how a man drives tells you everything you need to know about his character. Dad drove a '78 Ford pickup, manual transmission. He taught me how to drive stick in the parking lot of the old abandoned mall on the east side. That mall used to be the place to go, you know? They had a Sears, a JCPenney, and this incredible arcade where I spent half my allowance on Pac-Man. \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, right, the hunger. Feel better? I don't know if it makes me feel better about anything, really. I'm not even sure what would be bothering me to begin with. It's just... I just do it. 

You know, my buddy Dave down at the plant—he works the forklift on the loading dock—he says the same thing about his scratch-off tickets. I told him he should have had a mechanic look at it first, but you know Dave, he's always in a hurry. 

But yeah, the eating... I don't think it's about feeling better. It's just what happens when I pull into the garage.

Would I still do it? I mean... yeah, probably. I don't really think it has anything to do with stress from the plant. Even on a good day, I still end up out there in the garage with the food. 

Like last Friday, it was a totally easy shift. Usually on Fridays they bring in donuts for the floor. I don't even play golf, but still.

The guy next to me, Frank, he always complains about his back, but honestly, my back is fine. It's just the hours that get to you. Anyway, after those late shifts, I'll stop for takeout on the way home. I end up getting, like, two burgers, a large fry, a shake... and you'd think that'd be enough, right? But then I get home, and I go straight to the extra fridge we keep out in the garage. My wife wanted that fridge for Thanksgiving turkeys, but she barely uses it. So I'll stand out there in the garage and just clean out whatever leftovers are in there, like it's a whole second supper. It's just... I don't know, it's getting out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating that second supper in the garage?\n\nPatient: Control? No, not really. It’s more like... once I start, it feels like a switch is flipped. I’ll be standing out there in the cold, literally telling myself, 'Roy, shut the bag and go inside,' but my hands just keep reaching for more. \n\nAnd usually it's those giant bulk bags, you know? My wife, Linda, she gets them at Costco. She goes every other Saturday with her sister, Patty. They make a whole day of it, always get the hot dogs at the food court. I keep telling her, 'Linda, the kids are grown, it's just the two of us now, we don't need a fifty-pound bag of rice or those massive tubs of pretzels.' But she insists it’s a good deal because of the unit price. The salt from those pretzels is a nightmare, though—it gets all over the concrete out there. I actually had to sweep the whole garage floor last Sunday because it was tracking into the mudroom and scratching up the linoleum. \n\nBut, uh, what was I... right, the eating. Yeah, even when I know I should just zip the bag up and walk away, my hands just keep going back for another handful.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes in the garage?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. Afterward, I just stand there looking at the empty wrappers and I feel... I don't know, ashamed. Disgusted with myself, really. I mean, I've always been the dependable one, you know? Thirty years at the plant, never missed a shift unless I had a fever over 102. \n\nSpeaking of the plant, they just changed our punch-in system. Used to be these heavy paper cards—you'd hear that solid *clack* when you punched in. Now it's all digital badges. You just wave it near a sensor, and it makes this pathetic little beep. Half the time the sensor doesn't even read it right, and then you have to go see Brenda in HR. Brenda's nice enough, I guess, but she keeps her office freezing cold. She's got this little space heater under her desk, but it doesn't do a thing for the rest of the room. I was in there for twenty minutes last Tuesday trying to fix a missed punch, and my hands were practically numb by the time I left. \n\nBut, anyway, yeah, the eating. It just makes me feel like I'm sneaking around in my own house, you know? Like I'm letting myself down. I'm supposed to be disciplined, but out there in the garage... I just lose it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry when you start eating that second supper in the garage?\n\nPatient: Hungry? I mean... I don't really know if it's hunger. It's more just... I'm there, and the food's there. Honestly, half the time my stomach is already tight from the takeout I had on the drive home. \n\nYou know, the drive home is supposed to be the relaxing part of the night. But they've been doing construction on Route 9 for... gosh, it feels like three years now. They put up those orange barrels, and then nobody ever seems to actually be working. Just miles of barrels. Last week, this guy in a silver Honda tried to cut me off right where it merges down to one lane. I honked at him, and he had the nerve to flip me the bird! Can you believe that? People just have no patience anymore. My dad used to say that how a man drives tells you everything you need to know about his character. Dad drove a '78 Ford pickup, manual transmission. He taught me how to drive stick in the parking lot of the old abandoned mall on the east side. That mall used to be the place to go, you know? They had a Sears, a JCPenney, and this incredible arcade where I spent half my allowance on Pac-Man. \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, right, the hunger. Yeah, I don't think I'm actually hungry when I'm out in the garage. I just... I just eat anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before you start eating that second supper in the garage?\n\nPatient: Stressed? Anxious? I... I don't really know if I'd call it that. I don't feel like I'm upset when I go out there. It's just sort of my routine now, you know? After work, I pull in, and it's just what I do. \n\nSpeaking of routines, Linda has this whole evening routine she does now. She bought this fancy skincare set from some infomercial she saw at 2 AM. It's got like six different steps. First she uses this foaming stuff that smells like grapefruit, then some kind of toner, then a serum... I don't even know what a serum is, honestly. Looks like water, but she paid eighty bucks for a bottle the size of my thumb. And she stands in the bathroom for twenty minutes doing all this. Meanwhile, our bathroom sink has been draining slow for a month. I keep meaning to take the P-trap apart and clear it out. Probably just hair and soap scum built up in there. I bought a snake at Home Depot last weekend, but I haven't gotten around to using it. Home Depot was a madhouse, by the way. They had their spring garden sale going on, and people were fighting over bags of mulch. Literally yelling at each other over two-dollar bags of dirt. \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't think I'm upset or anything before I eat in the garage. I don't really notice feeling anything at all, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating that second supper in the garage helps you feel better about something that's been bothering you?\n\nPatient: Feel better? I don't know if it makes me feel better about anything, really. I'm not even sure what would be bothering me to begin with. It's just... I just do it. \n\nYou know, my buddy Dave down at the plant—he works the forklift on the loading dock—he says the same thing about his scratch-off tickets. He buys five of those twenty-dollar tickets every Friday when he cashes his check. I told him he's throwing his money away, but he says it's just something he does. Dave's a good guy, but he's got terrible luck. Last year he bought a used bass boat off Craigslist, thought he got the deal of the century. Took it out to Lake Minnetonka on a Saturday morning, got about a hundred yards from the launch, and the motor just completely seized up. Turns out the guy who sold it to him had run it without oil for who knows how long. Dave had to get towed back in by some teenagers on jet skis. He was so embarrassed. I told him he should have had a mechanic look at it first, but you know Dave, he's always in a hurry. \n\nBut yeah, the eating... I don't think it's about feeling better. It's just what happens when I pull into the garage.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat that second supper in the garage if you weren't feeling any stress or anxiety from work?\n\nPatient: Would I still do it? I mean... yeah, probably. I don't really think it has anything to do with stress from the plant. Even on a good day, I still end up out there in the garage with the food. \n\nLike last Friday, it was a totally easy shift. Usually on Fridays they bring in donuts for the floor. Well, they used to bring in the good ones from that independent bakery on 4th Street, but corporate cut the budget this year, so now it's just those waxy ones from the grocery store bakery aisle. You know the ones, where the chocolate frosting just peels off in one big sheet? Gary, our shift manager, he actually tried to fight corporate on it. Gary's a good guy. He's retiring next month, actually. Moving down to Florida... Naples, I think? Or maybe Fort Myers. Somewhere with a lot of golf courses. He bought this custom set of Callaway clubs that he won't stop talking about in the breakroom. Cost him like two grand. Linda would absolutely kill me if I spent two grand on golf clubs. I don't even play golf, but still. \n\nBut anyway... uh, what were we talking about? Work stress. Right. Yeah, I don't really think that's it. Whether work was a nightmare or a breeze, I just... I just eat the food either way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Stop myself? I... I don't think I can. It's like, once I start, it feels like a switch is flipped. I'll be sitting there, literally telling myself, "Roy, shut the bag and go inside," but my hands just keep reaching for more. I can't stop it. 

It's kind of like that old Chevy truck my dad used to have. A '78 C10, two-tone blue and white. Beautiful truck, but the throttle linkage would stick sometimes. You'd take your foot off the gas, but the engine would just keep revving, pulling you forward. He finally had to replace the whole carburetor. Bought a rebuilt Holley off a guy at a swap meet in Dayton. Those swap meets are crazy, by the way. People selling everything from rusty hubcaps to complete engine blocks. I bought a set of socket wrenches there once, Craftsman, back when they still made them in the USA. Best wrenches I ever owned, until my neighbor borrowed the 3/8ths drive and never gave it back. I should really ask him for that. He's always borrowing stuff. Last month it was my power washer. He said he was going to clean his deck, but I saw him using it to wash his wife's minivan. 

But yeah, the eating... I try to tell myself to stop, I really do. But it's like my hands have a mind of their own and I just keep going until it's all gone.

She's got this little space heater under her desk, but it doesn't do a thing for the rest of the room. I was in there for twenty minutes last Tuesday trying to fix a missed punch, and my hands were practically numb by the time I left. \n\nBut, anyway, yeah, the eating. Turns out the guy who sold it to him had run it without oil for who knows how long. Dave had to get towed back in by some teenagers on jet skis. He was so embarrassed. I told him he should have had a mechanic look at it first, but you know Dave, he's always in a hurry. \n\nBut yeah, the eating... I don't think it's about feeling better. It's just what happens when I pull into the garage.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat that second supper in the garage if you weren't feeling any stress or anxiety from work?\n\nPatient: Would I still do it? I mean... yeah, probably. I don't really think it has anything to do with stress from the plant. Even on a good day, I still end up out there in the garage with the food. \n\nLike last Friday, it was a totally easy shift. Usually on Fridays they bring in donuts for the floor. Well, they used to bring in the good ones from that independent bakery on 4th Street, but corporate cut the budget this year, so now it's just those waxy ones from the grocery store bakery aisle. You know the ones, where the chocolate frosting just peels off in one big sheet? Gary, our shift manager, he actually tried to fight corporate on it. Gary's a good guy. He's retiring next month, actually. Moving down to Florida... Naples, I think? Or maybe Fort Myers. Somewhere with a lot of golf courses. He bought this custom set of Callaway clubs that he won't stop talking about in the breakroom. Cost him like two grand. Linda would absolutely kill me if I spent two grand on golf clubs. I don't even play golf, but still. \n\nBut anyway... uh, what were we talking about? Work stress. Right. Yeah, I don't really think that's it. Whether work was a nightmare or a breeze, I just... I just eat the food either way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be unable to stop yourself from eating that second supper in the garage even if you tried?\n\nPatient: Stop myself? I... I don't think I can. It's like, once I start, it feels like a switch is flipped. I'll be sitting there, literally telling myself, \"Roy, shut the bag and go inside,\" but my hands just keep reaching for more. I can't stop it. \n\nIt's kind of like that old Chevy truck my dad used to have. A '78 C10, two-tone blue and white. Beautiful truck, but the throttle linkage would stick sometimes. You'd take your foot off the gas, but the engine would just keep revving, pulling you forward. He finally had to replace the whole carburetor. Bought a rebuilt Holley off a guy at a swap meet in Dayton. Those swap meets are crazy, by the way. People selling everything from rusty hubcaps to complete engine blocks. I bought a set of socket wrenches there once, Craftsman, back when they still made them in the USA. Best wrenches I ever owned, until my neighbor borrowed the 3/8ths drive and never gave it back. I should really ask him for that. He's always borrowing stuff. Last month it was my power washer. He said he was going to clean his deck, but I saw him using it to wash his wife's minivan. \n\nBut yeah, the eating... I try to tell myself to stop, I really do. But it's like my hands have a mind of their own and I just keep going until it's all gone.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you eat that second supper in the garage because you feel a strong urge to do so, or because it helps you deal with feelings or emotions?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / production_worker / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
An urge? Or, like, emotions? I... I don't really know. I don't think I'm dealing with emotions, really. I'm not a very emotional guy. Linda, my wife, she always says I have the emotional range of a spark plug. Which is funny, because I was actually just out in the garage changing the spark plugs on her Corolla last weekend. She's got that 2012 model, the one with the weird issue where the alternator whines if it gets too cold out. I told her we should just trade it in, maybe get one of those small SUVs. The RAV4s are nice. My buddy at the plant, Tom, his wife drives a RAV4 and they take it up to their cabin every summer. They've got a place up near Duluth. Beautiful up there, right on the water. They go walleye fishing all the time. I haven't been fishing in years. I used to go with my dad when I was a kid, but you know how it is, you get busy with work and the house and you just stop doing things you used to do. 

But, uh... sorry, what was the question? Oh, why I eat the food. Yeah, I don't really know if it's a strong urge or what. I just don't really have a good explanation for it.

Looks like water, but she paid eighty bucks for a bottle the size of my thumb. And she stands in the bathroom for twenty minutes doing all this. A strong urge? I guess... I mean, I don't really know how to describe it. It's not like I'm standing there shaking or anything. I just... I'm out there, and the food is there, and I eat it. 

I actually built those shelves in the garage myself, you know. The ones where I put the food down. Used three-quarter-inch plywood and heavy-duty L-brackets. You have to use the good brackets, otherwise they sag over time. I learned that the hard way when I built some shelving for Linda's gardening supplies a few years back. Put a couple of big bags of potting soil on there and the whole thing bowed in the middle. Looked like a hammock. I had to take it all down and start over. She was pretty annoyed because she couldn't get to her trowels for a week. She grows these amazing heirloom tomatoes, though. Cherokee Purples, I think they're called. Huge things, ugly as sin, but they taste incredible on a BLT. You really need thick-cut bacon for a good BLT, not that flimsy stuff that turns to dust when you fry it. 

But, uh... yeah, the urge thing. I don't know if it's a strong urge. I just do it. I don't really have a better way to explain it, to be honest.

I'm out there, and the food is there, and I eat it. \n\nI actually built those shelves in the garage myself, you know. The ones where I put the food down. Used three-quarter-inch plywood and heavy-duty L-brackets. You have to use the good brackets, otherwise they sag over time. I learned that the hard way when I built some shelving for Linda's gardening supplies a few years back. Put a couple of big bags of potting soil on there and the whole thing bowed in the middle. Looked like a hammock. I had to take it all down and start over. She was pretty annoyed because she couldn't get to her trowels for a week. She grows these amazing heirloom tomatoes, though. Cherokee Purples, I think they're called. Huge things, ugly as sin, but they taste incredible on a BLT. You really need thick-cut bacon for a good BLT, not that flimsy stuff that turns to dust when you fry it. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, the urge thing. I don't know if it's a strong urge. I just do it. I don't really have a better way to explain it, to be honest.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would eat that second supper in the garage if you were in a very good mood?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / production_worker / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A good mood? I... I don't know. Maybe? I guess it doesn't really matter what kind of mood I'm in. Good mood, bad mood... the food is still there. 

Like last Tuesday, I was in a great mood. We hit our production quota early at the plant, and the shift supervisor actually bought everyone donuts from that bakery over on 4th Street. Not the chain place, the independent one run by that Polish family. They make these paczkis—I think that's how you say it—they're like jelly donuts but denser, you know? Unbelievable. Anyway, I was feeling pretty good driving home, listening to the classic rock station. They played "Free Bird," the whole live version, which you never hear on the radio anymore because it's like fourteen minutes long. I remember seeing Skynyrd back in '87, or maybe it was '88. It was at the county fairgrounds, poured rain the entire time. We were standing in mud up to our ankles, but nobody cared. My buddy Dave lost a shoe in the mud and had to drive home barefoot. He drove a manual transmission, too, an old Datsun pickup, so shifting gears barefoot was a real pain for him. 

But yeah, even on a good day like that... I still stopped and picked up the extra food on the way home, and I still ate it in the garage. So I don't think my mood really changes it. It just happens.

See, I work these late shifts down at the plant—mostly line work, checking the seals on the packaging, which is mind-numbing, let me tell you. The guy next to me, Frank, he always complains about his back, but honestly, my back is fine. It's just the hours that get to you. Anyway, after those late shifts, I'll stop for takeout on the way home. I end up getting, like, two burgers, a large fry, a shake... and you'd think that'd be enough, right? But then I get home, and I go straight to the extra fridge we keep out in the garage. My wife wanted that fridge for Thanksgiving turkeys, but she barely uses it. So I'll stand out there in the garage and just clean out whatever leftovers are in there, like it's a whole second supper. It's just... I don't know, it's getting out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating that second supper in the garage?\n\nPatient: Control? No, not really. It’s more like... once I start, it feels like a switch is flipped. I’ll be standing out there in the cold, literally telling myself, 'Roy, shut the bag and go inside,' but my hands just keep reaching for more. \n\nAnd usually it's those giant bulk bags, you know? My wife, Linda, she gets them at Costco. She goes every other Saturday with her sister, Patty. They make a whole day of it, always get the hot dogs at the food court. I keep telling her, 'Linda, the kids are grown, it's just the two of us now, we don't need a fifty-pound bag of rice or those massive tubs of pretzels.' But she insists it’s a good deal because of the unit price. The salt from those pretzels is a nightmare, though—it gets all over the concrete out there. Yeah, even when I know I should just zip the bag up and walk away, my hands just keep going back for another handful.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes in the garage?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. Afterward, I just stand there looking at the empty wrappers and I feel... I don't know, ashamed. Disgusted with myself, really. I mean, I've always been the dependable one, you know? Thirty years at the plant, never missed a shift unless I had a fever over 102. \n\nSpeaking of the plant, they just changed our punch-in system. Used to be these heavy paper cards—you'd hear that solid *clack* when you punched in. Now it's all digital badges. You just wave it near a sensor, and it makes this pathetic little beep. Half the time the sensor doesn't even read it right, and then you have to go see Brenda in HR. Brenda's nice enough, I guess, but she keeps her office freezing cold. She's got this little space heater under her desk, but it doesn't do a thing for the rest of the room. I was in there for twenty minutes last Tuesday trying to fix a missed punch, and my hands were practically numb by the time I left. \n\nBut, anyway, yeah, the eating. It just makes me feel like I'm sneaking around in my own house, you know? Like I'm letting myself down. I'm supposed to be disciplined, but out there in the garage... I just lose it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry when you start eating that second supper in the garage?\n\nPatient: Hungry? I mean... I don't really know if it's hunger. It's more just... I'm there, and the food's there. Honestly, half the time my stomach is already tight from the takeout I had on the drive home. \n\nYou know, the drive home is supposed to be the relaxing part of the night. But they've been doing construction on Route 9 for... gosh, it feels like three years now. They put up those orange barrels, and then nobody ever seems to actually be working. Just miles of barrels. Last week, this guy in a silver Honda tried to cut me off right where it merges down to one lane. I honked at him, and he had the nerve to flip me the bird! Can you believe that? People just have no patience anymore. My dad used to say that how a man drives tells you everything you need to know about his character. Dad drove a '78 Ford pickup, manual transmission. He taught me how to drive stick in the parking lot of the old abandoned mall on the east side. That mall used to be the place to go, you know? They had a Sears, a JCPenney, and this incredible arcade where I spent half my allowance on Pac-Man. \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, right, the hunger. Yeah, I don't think I'm actually hungry when I'm out in the garage. I just... I just eat anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before you start eating that second supper in the garage?\n\nPatient: Stressed? Anxious? I... I don't really know if I'd call it that. I don't feel like I'm upset when I go out there. It's just sort of my routine now, you know? After work, I pull in, and it's just what I do. \n\nSpeaking of routines, Linda has this whole evening routine she does now. She bought this fancy skincare set from some infomercial she saw at 2 AM. It's got like six different steps. First she uses this foaming stuff that smells like grapefruit, then some kind of toner, then a serum... I don't even know what a serum is, honestly. Looks like water, but she paid eighty bucks for a bottle the size of my thumb. And she stands in the bathroom for twenty minutes doing all this. Meanwhile, our bathroom sink has been draining slow for a month. I keep meaning to take the P-trap apart and clear it out. Probably just hair and soap scum built up in there. I bought a snake at Home Depot last weekend, but I haven't gotten around to using it. Home Depot was a madhouse, by the way. They had their spring garden sale going on, and people were fighting over bags of mulch. Literally yelling at each other over two-dollar bags of dirt. \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't think I'm upset or anything before I eat in the garage. I don't really notice feeling anything at all, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating that second supper in the garage helps you feel better about something that's been bothering you?\n\nPatient: Feel better? I don't know if it makes me feel better about anything, really. I'm not even sure what would be bothering me to begin with. It's just... I just do it. \n\nYou know, my buddy Dave down at the plant—he works the forklift on the loading dock—he says the same thing about his scratch-off tickets. He buys five of those twenty-dollar tickets every Friday when he cashes his check. I told him he's throwing his money away, but he says it's just something he does. Dave's a good guy, but he's got terrible luck. Last year he bought a used bass boat off Craigslist, thought he got the deal of the century. Took it out to Lake Minnetonka on a Saturday morning, got about a hundred yards from the launch, and the motor just completely seized up. Turns out the guy who sold it to him had run it without oil for who knows how long. Dave had to get towed back in by some teenagers on jet skis. He was so embarrassed. I told him he should have had a mechanic look at it first, but you know Dave, he's always in a hurry. \n\nBut yeah, the eating... I don't think it's about feeling better. It's just what happens when I pull into the garage.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat that second supper in the garage if you weren't feeling any stress or anxiety from work?\n\nPatient: Would I still do it? I mean... yeah, probably. I don't really think it has anything to do with stress from the plant. Even on a good day, I still end up out there in the garage with the food. \n\nLike last Friday, it was a totally easy shift. Usually on Fridays they bring in donuts for the floor. Well, they used to bring in the good ones from that independent bakery on 4th Street, but corporate cut the budget this year, so now it's just those waxy ones from the grocery store bakery aisle. You know the ones, where the chocolate frosting just peels off in one big sheet? Gary, our shift manager, he actually tried to fight corporate on it. Gary's a good guy. He's retiring next month, actually. Moving down to Florida... Naples, I think? Or maybe Fort Myers. Somewhere with a lot of golf courses. He bought this custom set of Callaway clubs that he won't stop talking about in the breakroom. Cost him like two grand. Linda would absolutely kill me if I spent two grand on golf clubs. I don't even play golf, but still. \n\nBut anyway... uh, what were we talking about? Work stress. Right. Yeah, I don't really think that's it. Whether work was a nightmare or a breeze, I just... I just eat the food either way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be unable to stop yourself from eating that second supper in the garage even if you tried?\n\nPatient: Stop myself? I... I don't think I can. It's like, once I start, it feels like a switch is flipped. I'll be sitting there, literally telling myself, \"Roy, shut the bag and go inside,\" but my hands just keep reaching for more. I can't stop it. \n\nIt's kind of like that old Chevy truck my dad used to have. A '78 C10, two-tone blue and white. Beautiful truck, but the throttle linkage would stick sometimes. You'd take your foot off the gas, but the engine would just keep revving, pulling you forward. He finally had to replace the whole carburetor. Bought a rebuilt Holley off a guy at a swap meet in Dayton. Those swap meets are crazy, by the way. People selling everything from rusty hubcaps to complete engine blocks. I bought a set of socket wrenches there once, Craftsman, back when they still made them in the USA. Best wrenches I ever owned, until my neighbor borrowed the 3/8ths drive and never gave it back. I should really ask him for that. He's always borrowing stuff. Last month it was my power washer. He said he was going to clean his deck, but I saw him using it to wash his wife's minivan. \n\nBut yeah, the eating... I try to tell myself to stop, I really do. But it's like my hands have a mind of their own and I just keep going until it's all gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat that second supper in the garage because you feel a strong urge to do so, or because it helps you deal with feelings or emotions?\n\nPatient: An urge? Or, like, emotions? I... I don't really know. I don't think I'm dealing with emotions, really. I'm not a very emotional guy. Linda, my wife, she always says I have the emotional range of a spark plug. Which is funny, because I was actually just out in the garage changing the spark plugs on her Corolla last weekend. She's got that 2012 model, the one with the weird issue where the alternator whines if it gets too cold out. I told her we should just trade it in, maybe get one of those small SUVs. The RAV4s are nice. My buddy at the plant, Tom, his wife drives a RAV4 and they take it up to their cabin every summer. They've got a place up near Duluth. Beautiful up there, right on the water. They go walleye fishing all the time. I haven't been fishing in years. I used to go with my dad when I was a kid, but you know how it is, you get busy with work and the house and you just stop doing things you used to do. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what was the question? Oh, why I eat the food. Yeah, I don't really know if it's a strong urge or what. I just don't really have a good explanation for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a strong urge to eat when you're standing in the garage with the food?\n\nPatient: A strong urge? I guess... I mean, I don't really know how to describe it. It's not like I'm standing there shaking or anything. I just... I'm out there, and the food is there, and I eat it. \n\nI actually built those shelves in the garage myself, you know. The ones where I put the food down. Used three-quarter-inch plywood and heavy-duty L-brackets. You have to use the good brackets, otherwise they sag over time. I learned that the hard way when I built some shelving for Linda's gardening supplies a few years back. Put a couple of big bags of potting soil on there and the whole thing bowed in the middle. Looked like a hammock. I had to take it all down and start over. She was pretty annoyed because she couldn't get to her trowels for a week. She grows these amazing heirloom tomatoes, though. Cherokee Purples, I think they're called. Huge things, ugly as sin, but they taste incredible on a BLT. You really need thick-cut bacon for a good BLT, not that flimsy stuff that turns to dust when you fry it. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, the urge thing. I don't know if it's a strong urge. I just do it. I don't really have a better way to explain it, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat that second supper in the garage if you were in a very good mood?\n\nPatient: A good mood? I... I bought a set of socket wrenches there once, Craftsman, back when they still made them in the USA. Best wrenches I ever owned, until my neighbor borrowed the 3/8ths drive and never gave it back. I should really ask him for that. He's always borrowing stuff. Last month it was my power washer. He said he was going to clean his deck, but I saw him using it to wash his wife's minivan. \n\nBut yeah, the eating... I try to tell myself to stop, I really do. But it's like my hands have a mind of their own and I just keep going until it's all gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat that second supper in the garage because you feel a strong urge to do so, or because it helps you deal with feelings or emotions?\n\nPatient: An urge? Or, like, emotions? I... I don't really know. I don't think I'm dealing with emotions, really. I'm not a very emotional guy. Linda, my wife, she always says I have the emotional range of a spark plug. Which is funny, because I was actually just out in the garage changing the spark plugs on her Corolla last weekend. She's got that 2012 model, the one with the weird issue where the alternator whines if it gets too cold out. I told her we should just trade it in, maybe get one of those small SUVs. The RAV4s are nice. My buddy at the plant, Tom, his wife drives a RAV4 and they take it up to their cabin every summer. They've got a place up near Duluth. Beautiful up there, right on the water. They go walleye fishing all the time. I haven't been fishing in years. I used to go with my dad when I was a kid, but you know how it is, you get busy with work and the house and you just stop doing things you used to do. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what was the question? Oh, why I eat the food. Yeah, I don't really know if it's a strong urge or what. I just don't really have a good explanation for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a strong urge to eat when you're standing in the garage with the food?\n\nPatient: A strong urge? I guess... I mean, I don't really know how to describe it. It's not like I'm standing there shaking or anything. I just... I'm out there, and the food is there, and I eat it. \n\nI actually built those shelves in the garage myself, you know. The ones where I put the food down. Used three-quarter-inch plywood and heavy-duty L-brackets. You have to use the good brackets, otherwise they sag over time. I learned that the hard way when I built some shelving for Linda's gardening supplies a few years back. Put a couple of big bags of potting soil on there and the whole thing bowed in the middle. Looked like a hammock. I had to take it all down and start over. She was pretty annoyed because she couldn't get to her trowels for a week. She grows these amazing heirloom tomatoes, though. I don't know. Maybe? I guess it doesn't really matter what kind of mood I'm in. Good mood, bad mood... the food is still there. \n\nLike last Tuesday, I was in a great mood. Stress? I mean, I honestly don't know. I don't really consider myself a stressed-out guy to begin with. My job at the plant is pretty steady, and we've got a good union, so I don't worry about layoffs much. 

Actually, the guy who is stressed all the time is our union rep, Gary. He's always running around the floor with a clipboard, sweating through his collar. He just bought a new truck, too—a Ford F-150. Nice truck, but he got it in this weird bright yellow color. I told him it looks like a school bus. He should have gotten silver. Silver hides the road salt in the winter. They dump so much salt on the roads around here, it just eats right through your undercarriage if you're not careful. You gotta get that rust-proofing spray, otherwise your rocker panels are completely rusted out in three years. I usually get under my car every October and spray it down with Fluid Film. Smells terrible, kind of like wet sheep, but it works. You just have to make sure you don't get it on the exhaust or it smokes like crazy when you start the engine. 

But, uh... yeah, stress. Since I don't really feel like I have a lot of emotional issues or stress anyway, I guess taking it away wouldn't really change anything. I'd probably still be out there eating. I just really couldn't tell you why.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, what brings me in is... I guess it's my eating habits lately. See, I work these late shifts down at the plant—mostly line work, checking the seals on the packaging, which is mind-numbing, let me tell you. The guy next to me, Frank, he always complains about his back, but honestly, my back is fine. It's just the hours that get to you. Anyway, after those late shifts, I'll stop for takeout on the way home. I end up getting, like, two burgers, a large fry, a shake... and you'd think that'd be enough, right? But then I get home, and I go straight to the extra fridge we keep out in the garage. My wife wanted that fridge for Thanksgiving turkeys, but she barely uses it. So I'll stand out there in the garage and just clean out whatever leftovers are in there, like it's a whole second supper. It's just... I don't know, it's getting out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating that second supper in the garage?\n\nPatient: Control? No, not really. It’s more like... once I start, it feels like a switch is flipped. I’ll be standing out there in the cold, literally telling myself, 'Roy, shut the bag and go inside,' but my hands just keep reaching for more. \n\nAnd usually it's those giant bulk bags, you know? My wife, Linda, she gets them at Costco. She goes every other Saturday with her sister, Patty. They make a whole day of it, always get the hot dogs at the food court. I keep telling her, 'Linda, the kids are grown, it's just the two of us now, we don't need a fifty-pound bag of rice or those massive tubs of pretzels.' But she insists it’s a good deal because of the unit price. The salt from those pretzels is a nightmare, though—it gets all over the concrete out there. I actually had to sweep the whole garage floor last Sunday because it was tracking into the mudroom and scratching up the linoleum. \n\nBut, uh, what was I... right, the eating. Yeah, even when I know I should just zip the bag up and walk away, my hands just keep going back for another handful.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes in the garage?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. Afterward, I just stand there looking at the empty wrappers and I feel... I don't know, ashamed. Disgusted with myself, really. I mean, I've always been the dependable one, you know? Thirty years at the plant, never missed a shift unless I had a fever over 102. \n\nSpeaking of the plant, they just changed our punch-in system. Used to be these heavy paper cards—you'd hear that solid *clack* when you punched in. Now it's all digital badges. You just wave it near a sensor, and it makes this pathetic little beep. Half the time the sensor doesn't even read it right, and then you have to go see Brenda in HR. Brenda's nice enough, I guess, but she keeps her office freezing cold. She's got this little space heater under her desk, but it doesn't do a thing for the rest of the room. I was in there for twenty minutes last Tuesday trying to fix a missed punch, and my hands were practically numb by the time I left. \n\nBut, anyway, yeah, the eating. It just makes me feel like I'm sneaking around in my own house, you know? Like I'm letting myself down. I'm supposed to be disciplined, but out there in the garage... I just lose it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry when you start eating that second supper in the garage?\n\nPatient: Hungry? I mean... I don't really know if it's hunger. It's more just... I'm there, and the food's there. Honestly, half the time my stomach is already tight from the takeout I had on the drive home. \n\nYou know, the drive home is supposed to be the relaxing part of the night. But they've been doing construction on Route 9 for... gosh, it feels like three years now. They put up those orange barrels, and then nobody ever seems to actually be working. Just miles of barrels. Last week, this guy in a silver Honda tried to cut me off right where it merges down to one lane. I honked at him, and he had the nerve to flip me the bird! Can you believe that? People just have no patience anymore. My dad used to say that how a man drives tells you everything you need to know about his character. Dad drove a '78 Ford pickup, manual transmission. He taught me how to drive stick in the parking lot of the old abandoned mall on the east side. That mall used to be the place to go, you know? They had a Sears, a JCPenney, and this incredible arcade where I spent half my allowance on Pac-Man. \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, right, the hunger. Yeah, I don't think I'm actually hungry when I'm out in the garage. I just... I just eat anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before you start eating that second supper in the garage?\n\nPatient: Stressed? Anxious? I... I don't really know if I'd call it that. I don't feel like I'm upset when I go out there. It's just sort of my routine now, you know? After work, I pull in, and it's just what I do. \n\nSpeaking of routines, Linda has this whole evening routine she does now. She bought this fancy skincare set from some infomercial she saw at 2 AM. It's got like six different steps. First she uses this foaming stuff that smells like grapefruit, then some kind of toner, then a serum... I don't even know what a serum is, honestly. Looks like water, but she paid eighty bucks for a bottle the size of my thumb. And she stands in the bathroom for twenty minutes doing all this. Meanwhile, our bathroom sink has been draining slow for a month. I keep meaning to take the P-trap apart and clear it out. Probably just hair and soap scum built up in there. I bought a snake at Home Depot last weekend, but I haven't gotten around to using it. Home Depot was a madhouse, by the way. They had their spring garden sale going on, and people were fighting over bags of mulch. Literally yelling at each other over two-dollar bags of dirt. \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't think I'm upset or anything before I eat in the garage. I don't really notice feeling anything at all, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating that second supper in the garage helps you feel better about something that's been bothering you?\n\nPatient: Feel better? I don't know if it makes me feel better about anything, really. I'm not even sure what would be bothering me to begin with. It's just... I just do it. \n\nYou know, my buddy Dave down at the plant—he works the forklift on the loading dock—he says the same thing about his scratch-off tickets. He buys five of those twenty-dollar tickets every Friday when he cashes his check. I told him he's throwing his money away, but he says it's just something he does. Dave's a good guy, but he's got terrible luck. Last year he bought a used bass boat off Craigslist, thought he got the deal of the century. Took it out to Lake Minnetonka on a Saturday morning, got about a hundred yards from the launch, and the motor just completely seized up. Turns out the guy who sold it to him had run it without oil for who knows how long. Dave had to get towed back in by some teenagers on jet skis. He was so embarrassed. I told him he should have had a mechanic look at it first, but you know Dave, he's always in a hurry. \n\nBut yeah, the eating... I don't think it's about feeling better. It's just what happens when I pull into the garage.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat that second supper in the garage if you weren't feeling any stress or anxiety from work?\n\nPatient: Would I still do it? I mean... yeah, probably. I don't really think it has anything to do with stress from the plant. Even on a good day, I still end up out there in the garage with the food. \n\nLike last Friday, it was a totally easy shift. Usually on Fridays they bring in donuts for the floor. Well, they used to bring in the good ones from that independent bakery on 4th Street, but corporate cut the budget this year, so now it's just those waxy ones from the grocery store bakery aisle. You know the ones, where the chocolate frosting just peels off in one big sheet? Gary, our shift manager, he actually tried to fight corporate on it. Gary's a good guy. He's retiring next month, actually. Moving down to Florida... Naples, I think? Or maybe Fort Myers. Somewhere with a lot of golf courses. He bought this custom set of Callaway clubs that he won't stop talking about in the breakroom. Cost him like two grand. Linda would absolutely kill me if I spent two grand on golf clubs. I don't even play golf, but still. \n\nBut anyway... uh, what were we talking about? Work stress. Right. Yeah, I don't really think that's it. Whether work was a nightmare or a breeze, I just... I just eat the food either way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be unable to stop yourself from eating that second supper in the garage even if you tried?\n\nPatient: Stop myself? I... I don't think I can. It's like, once I start, it feels like a switch is flipped. I'll be sitting there, literally telling myself, \"Roy, shut the bag and go inside,\" but my hands just keep reaching for more. I can't stop it. \n\nIt's kind of like that old Chevy truck my dad used to have. A '78 C10, two-tone blue and white. Beautiful truck, but the throttle linkage would stick sometimes. You'd take your foot off the gas, but the engine would just keep revving, pulling you forward. He finally had to replace the whole carburetor. Bought a rebuilt Holley off a guy at a swap meet in Dayton. Those swap meets are crazy, by the way. People selling everything from rusty hubcaps to complete engine blocks. I bought a set of socket wrenches there once, Craftsman, back when they still made them in the USA. Best wrenches I ever owned, until my neighbor borrowed the 3/8ths drive and never gave it back. I should really ask him for that. He's always borrowing stuff. Last month it was my power washer. He said he was going to clean his deck, but I saw him using it to wash his wife's minivan. \n\nBut yeah, the eating... I try to tell myself to stop, I really do. But it's like my hands have a mind of their own and I just keep going until it's all gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat that second supper in the garage because you feel a strong urge to do so, or because it helps you deal with feelings or emotions?\n\nPatient: An urge? Or, like, emotions? I... I don't really know. I don't think I'm dealing with emotions, really. I'm not a very emotional guy. Linda, my wife, she always says I have the emotional range of a spark plug. Which is funny, because I was actually just out in the garage changing the spark plugs on her Corolla last weekend. She's got that 2012 model, the one with the weird issue where the alternator whines if it gets too cold out. I told her we should just trade it in, maybe get one of those small SUVs. The RAV4s are nice. My buddy at the plant, Tom, his wife drives a RAV4 and they take it up to their cabin every summer. They've got a place up near Duluth. Beautiful up there, right on the water. They go walleye fishing all the time. I haven't been fishing in years. I used to go with my dad when I was a kid, but you know how it is, you get busy with work and the house and you just stop doing things you used to do. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what was the question? Oh, why I eat the food. Yeah, I don't really know if it's a strong urge or what. I just don't really have a good explanation for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a strong urge to eat when you're standing in the garage with the food?\n\nPatient: A strong urge? I guess... I mean, I don't really know how to describe it. It's not like I'm standing there shaking or anything. I just... I'm out there, and the food is there, and I eat it. \n\nI actually built those shelves in the garage myself, you know. The ones where I put the food down. Used three-quarter-inch plywood and heavy-duty L-brackets. You have to use the good brackets, otherwise they sag over time. I learned that the hard way when I built some shelving for Linda's gardening supplies a few years back. Put a couple of big bags of potting soil on there and the whole thing bowed in the middle. Looked like a hammock. I had to take it all down and start over. She was pretty annoyed because she couldn't get to her trowels for a week. She grows these amazing heirloom tomatoes, though. Cherokee Purples, I think they're called. Huge things, ugly as sin, but they taste incredible on a BLT. You really need thick-cut bacon for a good BLT, not that flimsy stuff that turns to dust when you fry it. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, the urge thing. I don't know if it's a strong urge. I just do it. I don't know. Maybe? I guess it doesn't really matter what kind of mood I'm in. Good mood, bad mood... the food is still there. \n\nLike last Tuesday, I was in a great mood. We hit our production quota early at the plant, and the shift supervisor actually bought everyone donuts from that bakery over on 4th Street. Anyway, I was feeling pretty good driving home, listening to the classic rock station. They played \"Free Bird,\" the whole live version, which you never hear on the radio anymore because it's like fourteen minutes long. I remember seeing Skynyrd back in '87, or maybe it was '88. It was at the county fairgrounds, poured rain the entire time. We were standing in mud up to our ankles, but nobody cared. My buddy Dave lost a shoe in the mud and had to drive home barefoot. He drove a manual transmission, too, an old Datsun pickup, so shifting gears barefoot was a real pain for him. \n\nBut yeah, even on a good day like that... I still stopped and picked up the extra food on the way home, and I still ate it in the garage. So I don't think my mood really changes it. It just happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat in the garage if you didn't have any stress or emotional issues in your life?\n\nPatient: Stress? I mean, I honestly don't know. I don't really consider myself a stressed-out guy to begin with. Smells terrible, kind of like wet sheep, but it works. You just have to make sure you don't get it on the exhaust or it smokes like crazy when you start the engine. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, stress. I'm out there, and the food is there, and I eat it. \n\nI actually built those shelves in the garage myself, you know. The ones where I put the food down. Used three-quarter-inch plywood and heavy-duty L-brackets. You have to use the good brackets, otherwise they sag over time. I learned that the hard way when I built some shelving for Linda's gardening supplies a few years back. Put a couple of big bags of potting soil on there and the whole thing bowed in the middle. Looked like a hammock. I had to take it all down and start over. She was pretty annoyed because she couldn't get to her trowels for a week. She grows these amazing heirloom tomatoes, though. Cherokee Purples, I think they're called. Huge things, ugly as sin, but they taste incredible on a BLT. You really need thick-cut bacon for a good BLT, not that flimsy stuff that turns to dust when you fry it. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, the urge thing. I don't know if it's a strong urge. I just do it. I don't really have a better way to explain it, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat that second supper in the garage if you were in a very good mood?\n\nPatient: A good mood? I... I don't know. Maybe? I guess it doesn't really matter what kind of mood I'm in. Good mood, bad mood... the food is still there. \n\nLike last Tuesday, I was in a great mood. We hit our production quota early at the plant, and the shift supervisor actually bought everyone donuts from that bakery over on 4th Street. Not the chain place, the independent one run by that Polish family. They make these paczkis—I think that's how you say it—they're like jelly donuts but denser, you know? Unbelievable. Anyway, I was feeling pretty good driving home, listening to the classic rock station. They played \"Free Bird,\" the whole live version, which you never hear on the radio anymore because it's like fourteen minutes long. I remember seeing Skynyrd back in '87, or maybe it was '88. It was at the county fairgrounds, poured rain the entire time. We were standing in mud up to our ankles, but nobody cared. My buddy Dave lost a shoe in the mud and had to drive home barefoot. He drove a manual transmission, too, an old Datsun pickup, so shifting gears barefoot was a real pain for him. \n\nBut yeah, even on a good day like that... I still stopped and picked up the extra food on the way home, and I still ate it in the garage. So I don't think my mood really changes it. It just happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat in the garage if you didn't have any stress or emotional issues in your life?\n\nPatient: Stress? I mean, I honestly don't know. I don't really consider myself a stressed-out guy to begin with. My job at the plant is pretty steady, and we've got a good union, so I don't worry about layoffs much. \n\nActually, the guy who is stressed all the time is our union rep, Gary. He's always running around the floor with a clipboard, sweating through his collar. He just bought a new truck, too—a Ford F-150. Nice truck, but he got it in this weird bright yellow color. I told him it looks like a school bus. He should have gotten silver. Silver hides the road salt in the winter. They dump so much salt on the roads around here, it just eats right through your undercarriage if you're not careful. You gotta get that rust-proofing spray, otherwise your rocker panels are completely rusted out in three years. I usually get under my car every October and spray it down with Fluid Film. Smells terrible, kind of like wet sheep, but it works. You just have to make sure you don't get it on the exhaust or it smokes like crazy when you start the engine. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, stress. Since I don't really feel like I have a lot of emotional issues or stress anyway, I guess taking it away wouldn't really change anything. I'd probably still be out there eating. I just really couldn't tell you why.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would still eat in the garage if you weren't alone?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no. No way. If someone was out there, I wouldn't touch it. Like, at dinner with my wife, Linda, I'll actually act like I'm not that hungry. I'll just eat a little bit and say I had a big lunch at the plant or something. 

Which is tough sometimes because she's a really good cook. She makes this pot roast in the slow cooker, leaves it on low for like eight hours while we're at work. You come in the front door and the whole house just smells incredible. She puts those little red potatoes in there, the ones with the really thin skins? You don't even have to peel them, which is great because I hate peeling potatoes. When I was a kid, my mom used to make me peel a whole five-pound bag of Russets every Sunday. We had this old metal peeler that was dull as a butter knife, and I'd always end up scraping my knuckles on it. Took absolutely forever. I still have a little scar on my left thumb from slipping with that thing. Right on the knuckle, see? Never really faded. 

But anyway, yeah, I'll pretend I'm full at dinner. And then I'll wait until she's busy in the living room watching her house-flipping shows, or I'll head out to the truck or the garage to eat the rest. I just... I'd be really embarrassed for her to see how much food I'm actually putting away. So I make sure I'm by myself.

Not the chain place, the independent one run by that Polish family. They make these paczkis—I think that's how you say it—they're like jelly donuts but denser, you know? Unbelievable. Anyway, I was feeling pretty good driving home, listening to the classic rock station. They played \"Free Bird,\" the whole live version, which you never hear on the radio anymore because it's like fourteen minutes long. I remember seeing Skynyrd back in '87, or maybe it was '88. It was at the county fairgrounds, poured rain the entire time. We were standing in mud up to our ankles, but nobody cared. My buddy Dave lost a shoe in the mud and had to drive home barefoot. He drove a manual transmission, too, an old Datsun pickup, so shifting gears barefoot was a real pain for him. \n\nBut yeah, even on a good day like that... I still stopped and picked up the extra food on the way home, and I still ate it in the garage. So I don't think my mood really changes it. It just happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat in the garage if you didn't have any stress or emotional issues in your life?\n\nPatient: Stress? I mean, I honestly don't know. I don't really consider myself a stressed-out guy to begin with. My job at the plant is pretty steady, and we've got a good union, so I don't worry about layoffs much. \n\nActually, the guy who is stressed all the time is our union rep, Gary. He's always running around the floor with a clipboard, sweating through his collar. He just bought a new truck, too—a Ford F-150. Nice truck, but he got it in this weird bright yellow color. I told him it looks like a school bus. He should have gotten silver. Silver hides the road salt in the winter. They dump so much salt on the roads around here, it just eats right through your undercarriage if you're not careful. You gotta get that rust-proofing spray, otherwise your rocker panels are completely rusted out in three years. I usually get under my car every October and spray it down with Fluid Film. Smells terrible, kind of like wet sheep, but it works. You just have to make sure you don't get it on the exhaust or it smokes like crazy when you start the engine. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, stress. Since I don't really feel like I have a lot of emotional issues or stress anyway, I guess taking it away wouldn't really change anything. I'd probably still be out there eating. I just really couldn't tell you why.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat in the garage if you weren't alone?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No way. If someone was out there, I wouldn't touch it. Like, at dinner with my wife, Linda, I'll actually act like I'm not that hungry. I'll just eat a little bit and say I had a big lunch at the plant or something. \n\nWhich is tough sometimes because she's a really good cook. She makes this pot roast in the slow cooker, leaves it on low for like eight hours while we're at work. You come in the front door and the whole house just smells incredible. She puts those little red potatoes in there, the ones with the really thin skins? You don't even have to peel them, which is great because I hate peeling potatoes. When I was a kid, my mom used to make me peel a whole five-pound bag of Russets every Sunday. We had this old metal peeler that was dull as a butter knife, and I'd always end up scraping my knuckles on it. Took absolutely forever. I still have a little scar on my left thumb from slipping with that thing. Right on the knuckle, see? Never really faded. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I'll pretend I'm full at dinner. And then I'll wait until she's busy in the living room watching her house-flipping shows, or I'll head out to the truck or the garage to eat the rest. I just... I'd be really embarrassed for her to see how much food I'm actually putting away. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Compelled? Yeah, I guess you could say that. It's like... once I actually open the bag and start, it feels like a switch just gets flipped in my head. I'll be sitting out there, and I'm literally telling myself, "Roy, just shut the bag. Put it down and go inside." But my hands just keep reaching in for more. It's like I'm watching myself do it but I can't stop it. 

Kind of like when you're driving on ice, you know? You hit a patch of black ice and the truck starts sliding, and your brain is screaming at you not to slam on the brakes, steer into the skid, but your foot just stomps the brake pedal anyway. I spun out on Interstate 90 a few years back doing exactly that. Completely totaled my old Chevy Silverado. It was a shame, too, because I had just put new tires on it. Michelin Defenders. Best all-season tire you can buy, if you ask me. They cost a fortune, but they usually last a good sixty thousand miles. I bought them at this little tire shop down the road from the plant. The owner, Sal, he's a great guy. Always gives me a discount because I helped him fix his air compressor once. He's got this huge mastiff dog that just sleeps in the waiting room all day. Thing must weigh a hundred and fifty pounds. Snores louder than my wife, and that's saying something. 

But yeah, it's that same feeling. Like I'm completely out of control. My brain says stop, but my hands just keep going until all the food is gone.
