I learned that the hard way when I built some shelving for Linda's gardening supplies a few years back. Put a couple of big bags of potting soil on there and the whole thing bowed in the middle. Looked like a hammock. I had to take it all down and start over. She was pretty annoyed because she couldn't get to her trowels for a week. She grows these amazing heirloom tomatoes, though. Cherokee Purples, I think they're called. Huge things, ugly as sin, but they taste incredible on a BLT. You really need thick-cut bacon for a good BLT, not that flimsy stuff that turns to dust when you fry it. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, the urge thing. I don't know if it's a strong urge. I just do it. I don't really have a better way to explain it, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat that second supper in the garage if you were in a very good mood?\n\nPatient: A good mood? I... I don't know. Maybe? I guess it doesn't really matter what kind of mood I'm in. Good mood, bad mood... the food is still there. \n\nLike last Tuesday, I was in a great mood. We hit our production quota early at the plant, and the shift supervisor actually bought everyone donuts from that bakery over on 4th Street. Not the chain place, the independent one run by that Polish family. They make these paczkis—I think that's how you say it—they're like jelly donuts but denser, you know? Unbelievable. Anyway, I was feeling pretty good driving home, listening to the classic rock station. They played \"Free Bird,\" the whole live version, which you never hear on the radio anymore because it's like fourteen minutes long. I remember seeing Skynyrd back in '87, or maybe it was '88. It was at the county fairgrounds, poured rain the entire time. We were standing in mud up to our ankles, but nobody cared. My buddy Dave lost a shoe in the mud and had to drive home barefoot. He drove a manual transmission, too, an old Datsun pickup, so shifting gears barefoot was a real pain for him. \n\nBut yeah, even on a good day like that... I still stopped and picked up the extra food on the way home, and I still ate it in the garage. So I don't think my mood really changes it. It just happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat in the garage if you didn't have any stress or emotional issues in your life?\n\nPatient: Stress? I mean, I honestly don't know. I don't really consider myself a stressed-out guy to begin with. My job at the plant is pretty steady, and we've got a good union, so I don't worry about layoffs much. \n\nActually, the guy who is stressed all the time is our union rep, Gary. He's always running around the floor with a clipboard, sweating through his collar. He just bought a new truck, too—a Ford F-150. Nice truck, but he got it in this weird bright yellow color. I told him it looks like a school bus. He should have gotten silver. Silver hides the road salt in the winter. They dump so much salt on the roads around here, it just eats right through your undercarriage if you're not careful. You gotta get that rust-proofing spray, otherwise your rocker panels are completely rusted out in three years. I usually get under my car every October and spray it down with Fluid Film. Smells terrible, kind of like wet sheep, but it works. You just have to make sure you don't get it on the exhaust or it smokes like crazy when you start the engine. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, stress. Since I don't really feel like I have a lot of emotional issues or stress anyway, I guess taking it away wouldn't really change anything. I'd probably still be out there eating. I just really couldn't tell you why.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat in the garage if you weren't alone?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No way. If someone was out there, I wouldn't touch it. Like, at dinner with my wife, Linda, I'll actually act like I'm not that hungry. I'll just eat a little bit and say I had a big lunch at the plant or something. \n\nWhich is tough sometimes because she's a really good cook. She makes this pot roast in the slow cooker, leaves it on low for like eight hours while we're at work. You come in the front door and the whole house just smells incredible. She puts those little red potatoes in there, the ones with the really thin skins? You don't even have to peel them, which is great because I hate peeling potatoes. When I was a kid, my mom used to make me peel a whole five-pound bag of Russets every Sunday. We had this old metal peeler that was dull as a butter knife, and I'd always end up scraping my knuckles on it. Took absolutely forever. I still have a little scar on my left thumb from slipping with that thing. Right on the knuckle, see? Never really faded. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I'll pretend I'm full at dinner. And then I'll wait until she's busy in the living room watching her house-flipping shows, or I'll head out to the truck or the garage to eat the rest. I just... I'd be really embarrassed for her to see how much food I'm actually putting away. So I make sure I'm by myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to eat in the garage, like something is compelling you to do it?\n\nPatient: Compelled? Yeah, I guess you could say that. It's like... once I actually open the bag and start, it feels like a switch just gets flipped in my head. I'll be sitting out there, and I'm literally telling myself, \"Roy, just shut the bag. Put it down and go inside.\" But my hands just keep reaching in for more. It's like I'm watching myself do it but I can't stop it. \n\nKind of like when you're driving on ice, you know? You hit a patch of black ice and the truck starts sliding, and your brain is screaming at you not to slam on the brakes, steer into the skid, but your foot just stomps the brake pedal anyway. I spun out on Interstate 90 a few years back doing exactly that. Completely totaled my old Chevy Silverado. It was a shame, too, because I had just put new tires on it. Michelin Defenders. Best all-season tire you can buy, if you ask me. They cost a fortune, but they usually last a good sixty thousand miles. I bought them at this little tire shop down the road from the plant. The owner, Sal, he's a great guy. Always gives me a discount because I helped him fix his air compressor once. He's got this huge mastiff dog that just sleeps in the waiting room all day. Thing must weigh a hundred and fifty pounds. Snores louder than my wife, and that's saying something. \n\nBut yeah, it's that same feeling. Like I'm completely out of control. My brain says stop, but my hands just keep going until all the food is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to stop eating once you start if you were not alone in the garage?\n\nPatient: Well, if someone walked out there *after* I already started? Man, that's my worst nightmare. Because like I was just saying, once I actually get going, it really does feel like that switch is flipped. I can be sitting there literally telling myself, \"Roy, shut the bag and go inside,\" but my hands just keep reaching for more on their own. It's like being on autopilot. \n\nSpeaking of autopilot, my buddy Dave at the plant just bought one of those cars that practically drives itself. A Tesla, I think? Or maybe one of those new Hyundais. Anyway, he was showing me how he can just take his hands off the wheel on the highway. Sounds incredibly dangerous if you ask me. What if a deer jumps out? We get a lot of deer around here, especially down by the creek on Route 9. A guy on the second shift hit an eight-pointer last November, completely smashed the front end of his sedan. Took the insurance company three months to sort it out because the body shop couldn't get the parts. Everything is on backorder these days, I swear. I tried to buy a simple replacement filter for my shop vac last week and the guy at the hardware store told me it'd be a month. A month! For a piece of pleated paper. \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, if I could stop. I mean, if Linda walked in, I'd probably panic and try to throw a tarp over the wrappers or something. But the urge itself? Since my hands just keep going once that switch flips, I honestly don't know if I could just easily put it down. It really feels like I'm not the one in the driver's seat anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat in the garage if you weren't trying to avoid your wife seeing how much you eat?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying before, the whole reason I'm out there in the first place is because I'd be absolutely mortified if Linda saw the actual amount of food I'm putting away. If I wasn't so embarrassed about it, I wouldn't need to sneak out to the truck or wait around for her to get distracted by her shows. I mean, I sit there at dinner acting like I barely have an appetite, just picking at my plate, specifically so she won't see what I can really eat. \n\nSo if I wasn't trying to hide it? I guess I'd just stay inside. It's freezing in that garage anyway. Especially right now with the draft coming under the side door. I really need to replace the weather stripping on that thing. I actually went to Home Depot on Saturday to get some of that rubber sealing tape, but they moved the whole aisle around. Used to be right next to the lumber, but now it's over by the garden supplies for some reason. Makes no sense at all. I ended up talking to this guy in an orange apron for twenty minutes about it, and he didn't even know where it was. Turns out he was just a vendor for the power tools, not a regular employee. He was trying to sell me on this new Makita cordless drill. Nice drill, honestly, lithium-ion battery, brushless motor... but my old DeWalt works just fine. Had it for fifteen years. Dropped it off the roof twice when I was fixing the gutters and it still spins perfectly true. They don't make them like that anymore. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... if I wasn't so ashamed of how much I was eating, I definitely wouldn't be out there freezing next to my workbench. I only do it so she doesn't see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating in the garage is related to how you're feeling emotionally at the time?\n\nPatient: Emotionally? Huh. I'm not really sure. I haven't exactly noticed feeling any particular way before I head out to the garage. I mean, everybody gets stressed, right? \n\nLike at the plant lately, they brought in this new floor manager. Kid barely looks twenty-five, right out of some fancy business school. Comes walking in wearing these pristine Timberland steel-toed boots that have clearly never seen a single day of actual labor. Not a scuff on them. Anyway, he tried telling me how to calibrate the stamping press last Tuesday. I've been running that exact machine since he was in middle school! It's a two-hundred-ton hydraulic press, you don't just tweak the pressure valve because a spreadsheet tells you to. You have to check the fluid lines first. Last time some hotshot did that, we blew a main seal and hydraulic fluid sprayed thirty feet across the floor. Took the maintenance crew three days to get it all up. Smelled like burnt plastic in that whole wing for a solid week. My buddy Dave—the one I was telling you about with the car—he actually slipped in the puddle and nearly busted his tailbone. Had to go to the chiropractor for a month. \n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, my feelings. Yeah, I don't know. I just eat. I never really stopped to pay attention to what kind of mood I'm in when I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat in the garage if you weren't using it as a way to cope with your feelings or stress?\n\nPatient: Well, like I just said, I don't really think I'm doing it to cope with stress or feelings or anything like that. I just haven't noticed any connection there. So, I mean, yeah, I guess I'd still be out there in the garage either way, because the whole point is just to keep Linda from seeing it. \n\nSpeaking of the garage, I really need to get out there this weekend and reorganize my tool chest. It's a complete disaster right now. I let my nephew borrow a bunch of my socket wrenches when he was working on his dirt bike last month, and he just threw them all back in the top drawer without putting them back in the organizer. Drives me absolutely crazy. You open the drawer and it's just a jumbled mess of metal. I spent twenty minutes looking for a 10-millimeter socket the other day. It's always the 10-millimeter that goes missing, isn't it? I swear there's some kind of black hole in every garage that just sucks up 10-millimeter sockets. My brother-in-law says he buys them in bulk now, just keeps a whole jar of them on his workbench like peanuts. He's got this massive setup in his pole barn, insulated walls, an actual pellet stove...\n\nBut, uh, anyway. What I'm saying is, I don't think it's about my feelings. I just do it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So I make sure I'm by myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to eat in the garage, like something is compelling you to do it?\n\nPatient: Compelled? Yeah, I guess you could say that. It's like... once I actually open the bag and start, it feels like a switch just gets flipped in my head. I'll be sitting out there, and I'm literally telling myself, \"Roy, just shut the bag. Put it down and go inside.\" But my hands just keep reaching in for more. It's like I'm watching myself do it but I can't stop it. \n\nKind of like when you're driving on ice, you know? You hit a patch of black ice and the truck starts sliding, and your brain is screaming at you not to slam on the brakes, steer into the skid, but your foot just stomps the brake pedal anyway. I spun out on Interstate 90 a few years back doing exactly that. Completely totaled my old Chevy Silverado. It was a shame, too, because I had just put new tires on it. Michelin Defenders. Best all-season tire you can buy, if you ask me. They cost a fortune, but they usually last a good sixty thousand miles. I bought them at this little tire shop down the road from the plant. The owner, Sal, he's a great guy. Always gives me a discount because I helped him fix his air compressor once. He's got this huge mastiff dog that just sleeps in the waiting room all day. Thing must weigh a hundred and fifty pounds. Snores louder than my wife, and that's saying something. \n\nBut yeah, it's that same feeling. Like I'm completely out of control. My brain says stop, but my hands just keep going until all the food is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to stop eating once you start if you were not alone in the garage?\n\nPatient: Well, if someone walked out there *after* I already started? Man, that's my worst nightmare. Because like I was just saying, once I actually get going, it really does feel like that switch is flipped. I can be sitting there literally telling myself, \"Roy, shut the bag and go inside,\" but my hands just keep reaching for more on their own. It's like being on autopilot. \n\nSpeaking of autopilot, my buddy Dave at the plant just bought one of those cars that practically drives itself. A Tesla, I think? Or maybe one of those new Hyundais. Anyway, he was showing me how he can just take his hands off the wheel on the highway. Sounds incredibly dangerous if you ask me. What if a deer jumps out? We get a lot of deer around here, especially down by the creek on Route 9. A guy on the second shift hit an eight-pointer last November, completely smashed the front end of his sedan. Took the insurance company three months to sort it out because the body shop couldn't get the parts. Everything is on backorder these days, I swear. I tried to buy a simple replacement filter for my shop vac last week and the guy at the hardware store told me it'd be a month. A month! For a piece of pleated paper. \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, if I could stop. I mean, if Linda walked in, I'd probably panic and try to throw a tarp over the wrappers or something. But the urge itself? Since my hands just keep going once that switch flips, I honestly don't know if I could just easily put it down. It really feels like I'm not the one in the driver's seat anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat in the garage if you weren't trying to avoid your wife seeing how much you eat?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying before, the whole reason I'm out there in the first place is because I'd be absolutely mortified if Linda saw the actual amount of food I'm putting away. If I wasn't so embarrassed about it, I wouldn't need to sneak out to the truck or wait around for her to get distracted by her shows. I mean, I sit there at dinner acting like I barely have an appetite, just picking at my plate, specifically so she won't see what I can really eat. \n\nSo if I wasn't trying to hide it? I guess I'd just stay inside. It's freezing in that garage anyway. Especially right now with the draft coming under the side door. I really need to replace the weather stripping on that thing. I actually went to Home Depot on Saturday to get some of that rubber sealing tape, but they moved the whole aisle around. Used to be right next to the lumber, but now it's over by the garden supplies for some reason. Makes no sense at all. I ended up talking to this guy in an orange apron for twenty minutes about it, and he didn't even know where it was. Turns out he was just a vendor for the power tools, not a regular employee. He was trying to sell me on this new Makita cordless drill. Nice drill, honestly, lithium-ion battery, brushless motor... but my old DeWalt works just fine. Had it for fifteen years. Dropped it off the roof twice when I was fixing the gutters and it still spins perfectly true. They don't make them like that anymore. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... if I wasn't so ashamed of how much I was eating, I definitely wouldn't be out there freezing next to my workbench. I only do it so she doesn't see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating in the garage is related to how you're feeling emotionally at the time?\n\nPatient: Emotionally? Huh. I'm not really sure. I haven't exactly noticed feeling any particular way before I head out to the garage. I mean, everybody gets stressed, right? \n\nLike at the plant lately, they brought in this new floor manager. Kid barely looks twenty-five, right out of some fancy business school. Comes walking in wearing these pristine Timberland steel-toed boots that have clearly never seen a single day of actual labor. Not a scuff on them. Anyway, he tried telling me how to calibrate the stamping press last Tuesday. I've been running that exact machine since he was in middle school! It's a two-hundred-ton hydraulic press, you don't just tweak the pressure valve because a spreadsheet tells you to. You have to check the fluid lines first. Last time some hotshot did that, we blew a main seal and hydraulic fluid sprayed thirty feet across the floor. Took the maintenance crew three days to get it all up. Smelled like burnt plastic in that whole wing for a solid week. My buddy Dave—the one I was telling you about with the car—he actually slipped in the puddle and nearly busted his tailbone. Had to go to the chiropractor for a month. \n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, my feelings. Yeah, I don't know. I just eat. I never really stopped to pay attention to what kind of mood I'm in when I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat in the garage if you weren't using it as a way to cope with your feelings or stress?\n\nPatient: Well, like I just said, I don't really think I'm doing it to cope with stress or feelings or anything like that. I just haven't noticed any connection there. So, I mean, yeah, I guess I'd still be out there in the garage either way, because the whole point is just to keep Linda from seeing it. \n\nSpeaking of the garage, I really need to get out there this weekend and reorganize my tool chest. It's a complete disaster right now. I let my nephew borrow a bunch of my socket wrenches when he was working on his dirt bike last month, and he just threw them all back in the top drawer without putting them back in the organizer. Drives me absolutely crazy. You open the drawer and it's just a jumbled mess of metal. I spent twenty minutes looking for a 10-millimeter socket the other day. It's always the 10-millimeter that goes missing, isn't it? I swear there's some kind of black hole in every garage that just sucks up 10-millimeter sockets. My brother-in-law says he buys them in bulk now, just keeps a whole jar of them on his workbench like peanuts. He's got this massive setup in his pole barn, insulated walls, an actual pellet stove...\n\nBut, uh, anyway. What I'm saying is, I don't think it's about my feelings. I just do it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, yeah, like I was saying, I really don't think emotions have anything to do with it at all. So if I didn't feel anything, I'd still be out there. The only reason I'm in the garage is just so Linda doesn't catch me eating all that food.

See, I work these late shifts down at the plant—mostly line work, checking the seals on the packaging, which is mind-numbing, let me tell you. It’s more like... once I start, it feels like a switch is flipped. I keep telling her, 'Linda, the kids are grown, it's just the two of us now, we don't need a fifty-pound bag of rice or those massive tubs of pretzels.' But she insists it’s a good deal because of the unit price. The salt from those pretzels is a nightmare, though—it gets all over the concrete out there. Yeah... yeah, I do. Afterward, I just stand there looking at the empty wrappers and I feel... I don't know, ashamed. Disgusted with myself, really. I mean, I've always been the dependable one, you know? Thirty years at the plant, never missed a shift unless I had a fever over 102. \n\nSpeaking of the plant, they just changed our punch-in system. Half the time the sensor doesn't even read it right, and then you have to go see Brenda in HR. Brenda's nice enough, I guess, but she keeps her office freezing cold. She's got this little space heater under her desk, but it doesn't do a thing for the rest of the room. I was in there for twenty minutes last Tuesday trying to fix a missed punch, and my hands were practically numb by the time I left. \n\nBut, anyway, yeah, the eating. I told him he should have had a mechanic look at it first, but you know Dave, he's always in a hurry. \n\nBut yeah, the eating... I don't think it's about feeling better. It's just what happens when I pull into the garage.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat that second supper in the garage if you weren't feeling any stress or anxiety from work?\n\nPatient: Would I still do it? I mean... yeah, probably. I don't really think it has anything to do with stress from the plant. Even on a good day, I still end up out there in the garage with the food. \n\nLike last Friday, it was a totally easy shift. Usually on Fridays they bring in donuts for the floor. Well, they used to bring in the good ones from that independent bakery on 4th Street, but corporate cut the budget this year, so now it's just those waxy ones from the grocery store bakery aisle. You know the ones, where the chocolate frosting just peels off in one big sheet? Gary, our shift manager, he actually tried to fight corporate on it. Gary's a good guy. He's retiring next month, actually. Moving down to Florida... Naples, I think? Or maybe Fort Myers. Somewhere with a lot of golf courses. He bought this custom set of Callaway clubs that he won't stop talking about in the breakroom. Cost him like two grand. Linda would absolutely kill me if I spent two grand on golf clubs. I don't even play golf, but still. \n\nBut anyway... uh, what were we talking about? Work stress. Right. Yeah, I don't really think that's it. Whether work was a nightmare or a breeze, I just... I just eat the food either way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be unable to stop yourself from eating that second supper in the garage even if you tried?\n\nPatient: Stop myself? I... I don't think I can. It's like, once I start, it feels like a switch is flipped. I'll be sitting there, literally telling myself, \"Roy, shut the bag and go inside,\" but my hands just keep reaching for more. I can't stop it. \n\nIt's kind of like that old Chevy truck my dad used to have. A '78 C10, two-tone blue and white. Beautiful truck, but the throttle linkage would stick sometimes. You'd take your foot off the gas, but the engine would just keep revving, pulling you forward. He finally had to replace the whole carburetor. Bought a rebuilt Holley off a guy at a swap meet in Dayton. Those swap meets are crazy, by the way. People selling everything from rusty hubcaps to complete engine blocks. I bought a set of socket wrenches there once, Craftsman, back when they still made them in the USA. Best wrenches I ever owned, until my neighbor borrowed the 3/8ths drive and never gave it back. I should really ask him for that. He's always borrowing stuff. Last month it was my power washer. He said he was going to clean his deck, but I saw him using it to wash his wife's minivan. \n\nBut yeah, the eating... I try to tell myself to stop, I really do. But it's like my hands have a mind of their own and I just keep going until it's all gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat that second supper in the garage because you feel a strong urge to do so, or because it helps you deal with feelings or emotions?\n\nPatient: An urge? Or, like, emotions? I... I don't really know. I don't think I'm dealing with emotions, really. I'm not a very emotional guy. Linda, my wife, she always says I have the emotional range of a spark plug. Which is funny, because I was actually just out in the garage changing the spark plugs on her Corolla last weekend. She's got that 2012 model, the one with the weird issue where the alternator whines if it gets too cold out. I told her we should just trade it in, maybe get one of those small SUVs. The RAV4s are nice. My buddy at the plant, Tom, his wife drives a RAV4 and they take it up to their cabin every summer. They've got a place up near Duluth. Beautiful up there, right on the water. They go walleye fishing all the time. I haven't been fishing in years. I used to go with my dad when I was a kid, but you know how it is, you get busy with work and the house and you just stop doing things you used to do. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what was the question? Oh, why I eat the food. Yeah, I don't really know if it's a strong urge or what. I just don't really have a good explanation for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a strong urge to eat when you're standing in the garage with the food?\n\nPatient: A strong urge? I guess... I mean, I don't really know how to describe it. It's not like I'm standing there shaking or anything. I just... I'm out there, and the food is there, and I eat it. \n\nI actually built those shelves in the garage myself, you know. The ones where I put the food down. Used three-quarter-inch plywood and heavy-duty L-brackets. You have to use the good brackets, otherwise they sag over time. I learned that the hard way when I built some shelving for Linda's gardening supplies a few years back. Put a couple of big bags of potting soil on there and the whole thing bowed in the middle. Looked like a hammock. I had to take it all down and start over. She was pretty annoyed because she couldn't get to her trowels for a week. She grows these amazing heirloom tomatoes, though. Cherokee Purples, I think they're called. Huge things, ugly as sin, but they taste incredible on a BLT. You really need thick-cut bacon for a good BLT, not that flimsy stuff that turns to dust when you fry it. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, the urge thing. I don't know if it's a strong urge. I just do it. I don't really have a better way to explain it, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat that second supper in the garage if you were in a very good mood?\n\nPatient: A good mood? I... I don't know. Maybe? I guess it doesn't really matter what kind of mood I'm in. Good mood, bad mood... the food is still there. \n\nLike last Tuesday, I was in a great mood. We hit our production quota early at the plant, and the shift supervisor actually bought everyone donuts from that bakery over on 4th Street. Not the chain place, the independent one run by that Polish family. They make these paczkis—I think that's how you say it—they're like jelly donuts but denser, you know? Unbelievable. Anyway, I was feeling pretty good driving home, listening to the classic rock station. They played \"Free Bird,\" the whole live version, which you never hear on the radio anymore because it's like fourteen minutes long. I remember seeing Skynyrd back in '87, or maybe it was '88. It was at the county fairgrounds, poured rain the entire time. We were standing in mud up to our ankles, but nobody cared. My buddy Dave lost a shoe in the mud and had to drive home barefoot. He drove a manual transmission, too, an old Datsun pickup, so shifting gears barefoot was a real pain for him. \n\nBut yeah, even on a good day like that... I still stopped and picked up the extra food on the way home, and I still ate it in the garage. So I don't think my mood really changes it. It just happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat in the garage if you didn't have any stress or emotional issues in your life?\n\nPatient: Stress? I mean, I honestly don't know. I don't really consider myself a stressed-out guy to begin with. My job at the plant is pretty steady, and we've got a good union, so I don't worry about layoffs much. \n\nActually, the guy who is stressed all the time is our union rep, Gary. He's always running around the floor with a clipboard, sweating through his collar. He just bought a new truck, too—a Ford F-150. Nice truck, but he got it in this weird bright yellow color. I told him it looks like a school bus. He should have gotten silver. Silver hides the road salt in the winter. They dump so much salt on the roads around here, it just eats right through your undercarriage if you're not careful. You gotta get that rust-proofing spray, otherwise your rocker panels are completely rusted out in three years. I usually get under my car every October and spray it down with Fluid Film. Smells terrible, kind of like wet sheep, but it works. You just have to make sure you don't get it on the exhaust or it smokes like crazy when you start the engine. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, stress. Since I don't really feel like I have a lot of emotional issues or stress anyway, I guess taking it away wouldn't really change anything. I'd probably still be out there eating. I just really couldn't tell you why.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat in the garage if you weren't alone?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No way. If someone was out there, I wouldn't touch it. Like, at dinner with my wife, Linda, I'll actually act like I'm not that hungry. I'll just eat a little bit and say I had a big lunch at the plant or something. \n\nWhich is tough sometimes because she's a really good cook. She makes this pot roast in the slow cooker, leaves it on low for like eight hours while we're at work. You come in the front door and the whole house just smells incredible. She puts those little red potatoes in there, the ones with the really thin skins? You don't even have to peel them, which is great because I hate peeling potatoes. When I was a kid, my mom used to make me peel a whole five-pound bag of Russets every Sunday. We had this old metal peeler that was dull as a butter knife, and I'd always end up scraping my knuckles on it. Took absolutely forever. I still have a little scar on my left thumb from slipping with that thing. Right on the knuckle, see? Never really faded. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I'll pretend I'm full at dinner. And then I'll wait until she's busy in the living room watching her house-flipping shows, or I'll head out to the truck or the garage to eat the rest. I just... I'd be really embarrassed for her to see how much food I'm actually putting away. So I make sure I'm by myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to eat in the garage, like something is compelling you to do it?\n\nPatient: Compelled? Yeah, I guess you could say that. It's like... once I actually open the bag and start, it feels like a switch just gets flipped in my head. I'll be sitting out there, and I'm literally telling myself, \"Roy, just shut the bag. Put it down and go inside.\" But my hands just keep reaching in for more. It's like I'm watching myself do it but I can't stop it. \n\nKind of like when you're driving on ice, you know? You hit a patch of black ice and the truck starts sliding, and your brain is screaming at you not to slam on the brakes, steer into the skid, but your foot just stomps the brake pedal anyway. I spun out on Interstate 90 a few years back doing exactly that. Completely totaled my old Chevy Silverado. It was a shame, too, because I had just put new tires on it. Michelin Defenders. Best all-season tire you can buy, if you ask me. They cost a fortune, but they usually last a good sixty thousand miles. I bought them at this little tire shop down the road from the plant. The owner, Sal, he's a great guy. Always gives me a discount because I helped him fix his air compressor once. He's got this huge mastiff dog that just sleeps in the waiting room all day. Thing must weigh a hundred and fifty pounds. Snores louder than my wife, and that's saying something. \n\nBut yeah, it's that same feeling. Like I'm completely out of control. My brain says stop, but my hands just keep going until all the food is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to stop eating once you start if you were not alone in the garage?\n\nPatient: Well, if someone walked out there *after* I already started? Man, that's my worst nightmare. Because like I was just saying, once I actually get going, it really does feel like that switch is flipped. I can be sitting there literally telling myself, \"Roy, shut the bag and go inside,\" but my hands just keep reaching for more on their own. It's like being on autopilot. \n\nSpeaking of autopilot, my buddy Dave at the plant just bought one of those cars that practically drives itself. A Tesla, I think? Or maybe one of those new Hyundais. Anyway, he was showing me how he can just take his hands off the wheel on the highway. Sounds incredibly dangerous if you ask me. What if a deer jumps out? We get a lot of deer around here, especially down by the creek on Route 9. A guy on the second shift hit an eight-pointer last November, completely smashed the front end of his sedan. Took the insurance company three months to sort it out because the body shop couldn't get the parts. Everything is on backorder these days, I swear. I tried to buy a simple replacement filter for my shop vac last week and the guy at the hardware store told me it'd be a month. A month! For a piece of pleated paper. \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, if I could stop. I mean, if Linda walked in, I'd probably panic and try to throw a tarp over the wrappers or something. But the urge itself? Since my hands just keep going once that switch flips, I honestly don't know if I could just easily put it down. It really feels like I'm not the one in the driver's seat anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat in the garage if you weren't trying to avoid your wife seeing how much you eat?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying before, the whole reason I'm out there in the first place is because I'd be absolutely mortified if Linda saw the actual amount of food I'm putting away. If I wasn't so embarrassed about it, I wouldn't need to sneak out to the truck or wait around for her to get distracted by her shows. I mean, I sit there at dinner acting like I barely have an appetite, just picking at my plate, specifically so she won't see what I can really eat. \n\nSo if I wasn't trying to hide it? I guess I'd just stay inside. It's freezing in that garage anyway. Especially right now with the draft coming under the side door. I really need to replace the weather stripping on that thing. I actually went to Home Depot on Saturday to get some of that rubber sealing tape, but they moved the whole aisle around. Used to be right next to the lumber, but now it's over by the garden supplies for some reason. Makes no sense at all. I ended up talking to this guy in an orange apron for twenty minutes about it, and he didn't even know where it was. Turns out he was just a vendor for the power tools, not a regular employee. He was trying to sell me on this new Makita cordless drill. Nice drill, honestly, lithium-ion battery, brushless motor... but my old DeWalt works just fine. Had it for fifteen years. Dropped it off the roof twice when I was fixing the gutters and it still spins perfectly true. They don't make them like that anymore. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... if I wasn't so ashamed of how much I was eating, I definitely wouldn't be out there freezing next to my workbench. I only do it so she doesn't see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating in the garage is related to how you're feeling emotionally at the time?\n\nPatient: Emotionally? Huh. I'm not really sure. I haven't exactly noticed feeling any particular way before I head out to the garage. I mean, everybody gets stressed, right? \n\nLike at the plant lately, they brought in this new floor manager. Kid barely looks twenty-five, right out of some fancy business school. Comes walking in wearing these pristine Timberland steel-toed boots that have clearly never seen a single day of actual labor. Not a scuff on them. Anyway, he tried telling me how to calibrate the stamping press last Tuesday. I've been running that exact machine since he was in middle school! It's a two-hundred-ton hydraulic press, you don't just tweak the pressure valve because a spreadsheet tells you to. You have to check the fluid lines first. Last time some hotshot did that, we blew a main seal and hydraulic fluid sprayed thirty feet across the floor. Took the maintenance crew three days to get it all up. Smelled like burnt plastic in that whole wing for a solid week. My buddy Dave—the one I was telling you about with the car—he actually slipped in the puddle and nearly busted his tailbone. Had to go to the chiropractor for a month. \n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, my feelings. Yeah, I don't know. I just eat. I never really stopped to pay attention to what kind of mood I'm in when I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat in the garage if you weren't using it as a way to cope with your feelings or stress?\n\nPatient: Well, like I just said, I don't really think I'm doing it to cope with stress or feelings or anything like that. I just haven't noticed any connection there. So, I mean, yeah, I guess I'd still be out there in the garage either way, because the whole point is just to keep Linda from seeing it. \n\nSpeaking of the garage, I really need to get out there this weekend and reorganize my tool chest. It's a complete disaster right now. I let my nephew borrow a bunch of my socket wrenches when he was working on his dirt bike last month, and he just threw them all back in the top drawer without putting them back in the organizer. Drives me absolutely crazy. You open the drawer and it's just a jumbled mess of metal. I spent twenty minutes looking for a 10-millimeter socket the other day. It's always the 10-millimeter that goes missing, isn't it? I swear there's some kind of black hole in every garage that just sucks up 10-millimeter sockets. My brother-in-law says he buys them in bulk now, just keeps a whole jar of them on his workbench like peanuts. He's got this massive setup in his pole barn, insulated walls, an actual pellet stove...\n\nBut, uh, anyway. What I'm saying is, I don't think it's about my feelings. I just do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat in the garage if you weren't feeling any emotions at all?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was saying, I really don't think emotions have anything to do with it at all. So if I didn't feel anything, I'd still be out there. The only reason I'm in the garage is just so Linda doesn't catch me eating all that food. \n\nHonestly, if I didn't have to hide it, I'd be inside where it's warm. It gets so drafty out there. I was actually thinking about getting one of those little space heaters for the corner by the workbench. The guys at the plant were talking about these ceramic ones that don't use up too much electricity. You gotta be careful with space heaters, though. My neighbor, Gary, he left one running in his shed a couple winters ago and the whole thing went up in flames. Burned his riding mower right down to the frame. It was a John Deere, too, one of the older models with the good transmission. He was furious. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Habit? I mean, maybe. I don't really know if I'd call it a habit, but like I've been saying, it's definitely not about my feelings. I just... I do it because I don't want Linda to see. That's the only reason I'm out there. 

Speaking of habits, though, my dog, Buster, he's got this terrible habit of digging under the fence right by the rose bushes. Linda is about ready to string him up. She planted these heirloom tea roses last spring—ordered them out of some fancy catalog from Oregon, I think. Paid an arm and a leg for shipping. Anyway, Buster keeps trying to bury his rawhide bones right in the mulch. I've tried putting chicken wire down, I've tried those little decorative border fences, but he just pushes right past them. He's a Golden Retriever mix, so he's got some weight to him. Sweetest dog in the world, wouldn't hurt a fly, but man, he is stubborn. The other day he came inside with mud all over his nose, looked me dead in the eye, and sneezed dirt all over the kitchen floor. Linda had just mopped, too. I had to scramble to wipe it up before she came down from upstairs. Used half a roll of paper towels. 

But, uh... yeah. I don't really think about it being a habit or feelings or whatever. I just go to the garage so I can eat in private.

Drives me absolutely crazy. You open the drawer and it's just a jumbled mess of metal. I spent twenty minutes looking for a 10-millimeter socket the other day. It's always the 10-millimeter that goes missing, isn't it? I swear there's some kind of black hole in every garage that just sucks up 10-millimeter sockets. My brother-in-law says he buys them in bulk now, just keeps a whole jar of them on his workbench like peanuts. He's got this massive setup in his pole barn, insulated walls, an actual pellet stove...\n\nBut, uh, anyway. What I'm saying is, I don't think it's about my feelings. I just do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat in the garage if you weren't feeling any emotions at all?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was saying, I really don't think emotions have anything to do with it at all. So if I didn't feel anything, I'd still be out there. The only reason I'm in the garage is just so Linda doesn't catch me eating all that food. \n\nHonestly, if I didn't have to hide it, I'd be inside where it's warm. It gets so drafty out there. I was actually thinking about getting one of those little space heaters for the corner by the workbench. The guys at the plant were talking about these ceramic ones that don't use up too much electricity. You gotta be careful with space heaters, though. My neighbor, Gary, he left one running in his shed a couple winters ago and the whole thing went up in flames. Burned his riding mower right down to the frame. It was a John Deere, too, one of the older models with the good transmission. He was furious. Fought with the insurance company for six months because they tried to say it was negligence. \n\nBut, uh, yeah. It's not about feelings or anything. I just don't want my wife to see me eat.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating in the garage is something you do out of habit or because of how you're feeling at the time?\n\nPatient: Habit? I mean, maybe. I don't really know if I'd call it a habit, but like I've been saying, it's definitely not about my feelings. I just... I do it because I don't want Linda to see. That's the only reason I'm out there. \n\nSpeaking of habits, though, my dog, Buster, he's got this terrible habit of digging under the fence right by the rose bushes. Linda is about ready to string him up. She planted these heirloom tea roses last spring—ordered them out of some fancy catalog from Oregon, I think. Paid an arm and a leg for shipping. Anyway, Buster keeps trying to bury his rawhide bones right in the mulch. I've tried putting chicken wire down, I've tried those little decorative border fences, but he just pushes right past them. He's a Golden Retriever mix, so he's got some weight to him. Sweetest dog in the world, wouldn't hurt a fly, but man, he is stubborn. The other day he came inside with mud all over his nose, looked me dead in the eye, and sneezed dirt all over the kitchen floor. Linda had just mopped, too. I had to scramble to wipe it up before she came down from upstairs. Used half a roll of paper towels. \n\nBut, uh... yeah. I don't really think about it being a habit or feelings or whatever. I just go to the garage so I can eat in private."
  }
]
